Mar 2022</li><li class="metadata__item">5<!-- --> Min Read</li></ul><a class="primary-button social-icon social-icon--linkedin" target="_blank" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" class="linkedin_svg__responsive-icon" viewBox="0 0 48 48"><g clip-path="url(#linkedin_svg__a)"><path fill="currentColor" d="M44.447 0H3.544C1.584 0 0 1.547 0 3.46V44.53C0 46.444 1.584 48 3.544 48h40.903C46.407 48 48 46.444 48 44.54V3.46C48 1.546 46.406 0 44.447 0M14.24 40.903H7.116V17.991h7.125zM10.678 14.87a4.127 4.127 0 0 1-4.134-4.125 4.127 4.127 0 0 1 4.134-4.125 4.125 4.125 0 0 1 0 8.25m30.225 26.034h-7.115V29.766c0-2.653-.047-6.075-3.704-6.075-3.703 0-4.265 2.896-4.265 5.887v11.325h-7.107V17.991h6.826v3.13h.093c.947-1.8 3.272-3.702 class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>This is the concluding part of our in-depth analysis of Backdoor. Daxin, advanced malware that is being used by a China-linked espionage group.</p>

<p>In this blog, we will analyze the communications and network features of the malware.</p>

<h2>Communications protocol</h2>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing">In our <a href="https://symantec-enterprise-blogs.security.com/blogs/threat-intelligence/daxin-malware-espionage-analysis">previous blog</a>, we set up a lab consisting of four separate networks and five machines. Some of the machines had two network interfaces to communicate with different networks, but all packet forwarding functionality was disabled. Each machine ran various network services that were reachable from its neighbors only.</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-image-inline prose"><figure class="paragraph-image-inline__figure"><img alt="Figure 1. Test setup to illustrate Daxin’s backdoor capabilities." loading="lazy" width="751" height="758" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" style="color:transparent" srcSet="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2022-03%2FFig1_1.png.webp%3Fitok%3DzR0kKFxx%201x&amp;w=828&amp;q=75 1x, /_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2022-03%2FFig1_1.png.webp%3Fitok%3DzR0kKFxx%201x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75 2x" src="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2022-03%2FFig1_1.png.webp%3Fitok%3DzR0kKFxx%201x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75"/><figcaption class="paragraph-image-inline__caption">Figure 1. Test setup to illustrate Daxin’s backdoor capabilities.</figcaption></figure></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p class="MsoNoSpacing">In this section we will dissect the network traffic between two backdoor instances running on the separate computers “Alice-PC” and “Bob-PC”. The traffic was initiated by the Daxin backdoor running on “Alice-PC” when it was instructed to create a communication channel to “Dave-PC” passing via two intermediate nodes, “Bob-PC” and “Charlie-PC”, as described previously.</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-image-inline prose"><figure class="paragraph-image-inline__figure"><img alt="Figure 2. Wireshark capture of traffic between two backdoor instances. The screenshot and examples below are reused from a private report prepared by us that discussed an earlier sample, so certain details may not match. " loading="lazy" width="730" height="1050" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" style="color:transparent" srcSet="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2022-03%2FFig2.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3D2Rkh1dXm%201x&amp;w=750&amp;q=75 1x, /_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2022-03%2FFig2.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3D2Rkh1dXm%201x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75 2x" src="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2022-03%2FFig2.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3D2Rkh1dXm%201x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75"/><figcaption class="paragraph-image-inline__caption">Figure 2. Wireshark capture of traffic between two backdoor instances. The screenshot and examples below are reused from a private report prepared by us that discussed an earlier sample, so certain details may not match. </figcaption></figure></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p class="MsoNoSpacing">Using Wireshark, we captured traffic between two backdoor instances, one running on “Alice-PC” and the other on “Bob-PC”, as shown in Figure 2.</p>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing">Starting with the key exchange, all backdoor communication is carried out by exchanging messages that follow the same underlying format:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-keyword">import</span> struct

<span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">dissect_message</span>(<span class="hljs-params">message</span>):
    magic, kind, unknown_03, total_length = struct.unpack(<span class="hljs-string">&quot;&lt;HBBI&quot;</span>, message[: <span class="hljs-number">8</span>])
    <span class="hljs-keyword">assert</span> total_length == <span class="hljs-built_in">len</span>(message)
    
    <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;0000	magic			= <span class="hljs-subst">{magic:04x}</span>&quot;</span>)
    <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;0002	kind			= <span class="hljs-subst">{kind:02x}</span>&quot;</span>)
    <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;0003	unknown_03		= <span class="hljs-subst">{unknown_03:02x}</span>&quot;</span>)
    <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;0004	total_length	= <span class="hljs-subst">{total_length:08x}</span>&quot;</span>)
    
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> magic, kind, message[<span class="hljs-number">8</span>: ]
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>The <em>magic</em> value is always 0x9910 or 0x9911.</p>

<p>The <em>kind</em> value identifies the state transition during key exchange. Then, once the encrypted communication channel is established, it encodes the purpose of each message and determines the formatting of the data that follows the fixed-size header.</p>

<p>The initial message of the key exchange in the Wireshark capture is not encrypted:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-keyword">class</span> <span class="hljs-title class_">Session</span>:
    <span class="hljs-keyword">pass</span>

<span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">decode_key_exchange_1_message</span>(<span class="hljs-params">tcp_dump</span>):
    message = <span class="hljs-built_in">bytes</span>.fromhex(tcp_dump)
    magic, kind, message_body = dissect_message(message)
    <span class="hljs-keyword">assert</span> magic == <span class="hljs-number">0x9910</span>
    <span class="hljs-keyword">assert</span> kind <span class="hljs-keyword">in</span> [<span class="hljs-number">0x10</span>, <span class="hljs-number">0x11</span>]
    
    <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;0008	message_body (unused)	= <span class="hljs-subst">{message_body}</span>&quot;</span>)
    
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> Session()

my_session = decode_key_exchange_1_message(<span class="hljs-string">&quot;&quot;&quot;
    10 99 11 00 08 00 00 00
&quot;&quot;&quot;</span>)
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>It can be decoded as follows:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-number">0000	</span><span class="hljs-string">magic</span>				<span class="hljs-string">=</span> <span class="hljs-number">9910</span>
<span class="hljs-number">0002	</span><span class="hljs-string">kind</span> 				<span class="hljs-string">=</span> <span class="hljs-number">11</span>
<span class="hljs-number">0003	</span><span class="hljs-string">unknown_03</span>			<span class="hljs-string">=</span> <span class="hljs-number">00</span>
<span class="hljs-number">0004	</span><span class="hljs-string">total_length</span>		<span class="hljs-string">=</span> <span class="hljs-number">00000008</span>
<span class="hljs-number">0008	</span><span class="hljs-string">message_body</span> <span class="hljs-string">(unused)</span>	<span class="hljs-string">=</span> <span class="hljs-string">b&#x27;&#x27;</span>
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>The fields <em>magic</em> and <em>kind</em> correspond to the first three bytes of TCP data, 0x10 0x99 0x11. On the target computer, in case it is infected with a copy of the malicious driver, this sequence causes the TCP connection to be hijacked, as explained in <a href="https://symantec-enterprise-blogs.security.com/blogs/threat-intelligence/daxin-malware-espionage-analysis">part one of this blog series</a>.</p>

<p>The target checks that the received message is valid according to the session state machine, ensuring that <em>magic</em> is the expected constant 0x9910 and <em>kind</em> matches any of two supported values: 0x10 or 0x11. Next, it generates a nonce to use when encrypting any future incoming messages. Finally, it sends a response message with the nonce, its own details, and the information about the infected machine.</p>

<p>Parts of the response message are encrypted using a combination of the following algorithms:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-keyword">import</span> hashlib
<span class="hljs-keyword">import</span> itertools

<span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">rc4_variant</span>(<span class="hljs-params">key</span>):
    <span class="hljs-string">&quot;&quot;&quot;Variant of RC4 with modified initial value of j in PRGA.
    
    The initial value of j in PRGA is from the final KSA operation and may not be zero.
    &quot;&quot;&quot;</span>
    
    S = <span class="hljs-built_in">bytearray</span>(<span class="hljs-built_in">range</span>(<span class="hljs-number">0x100</span>))
    j = <span class="hljs-number">0</span>
    cycled_key = itertools.cycle(key)
    <span class="hljs-keyword">for</span> i <span class="hljs-keyword">in</span> <span class="hljs-built_in">range</span>(<span class="hljs-number">0x100</span>):
        j = (j + S[i] + <span class="hljs-built_in">next</span>(cycled_key)) &amp; <span class="hljs-number">0xff</span>
        S[i], S[j] = S[j], S[i]
    
    i = <span class="hljs-number">0</span>
    <span class="hljs-comment"># skipping j reinitialization</span>
    <span class="hljs-keyword">while</span> <span class="hljs-literal">True</span>:
        i = (i + <span class="hljs-number">1</span>) &amp; <span class="hljs-number">0xff</span>
        j = (j + S[i]) &amp; <span class="hljs-number">0xff</span>
        S[i], S[j] = S[j], S[i]
        K = S[(S[i] + S[j]) &amp; <span class="hljs-number">0xff</span>]
        <span class="hljs-keyword">yield</span> K

<span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">rol</span>(<span class="hljs-params">value, count, width=<span class="hljs-number">8</span></span>):
    mask = (<span class="hljs-number">1</span> &lt;&lt; width) - <span class="hljs-number">1</span>
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> (((value &lt;&lt; count) &amp; mask)
        | ((value &amp; mask) &gt;&gt; (width - count))
    )

<span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">xor_crypt</span>(<span class="hljs-params">data, key_stream</span>):
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> <span class="hljs-built_in">bytes</span>([byte ^ <span class="hljs-built_in">next</span>(key_stream) <span class="hljs-keyword">for</span> byte <span class="hljs-keyword">in</span> data])

<span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">derive_key</span>(<span class="hljs-params">nonce</span>):
    md5 = hashlib.md5()
    md5.update([REDACTED])
    md5.update(nonce)
    
    rc4_variant_key = <span class="hljs-built_in">bytearray</span>()
    <span class="hljs-keyword">for</span> byte <span class="hljs-keyword">in</span> md5.digest():
        rc4_variant_key.append([REDACTED])    
    rc4_variant_stream = rc4_variant(rc4_variant_key)
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> xor_crypt(nonce, rc4_variant_stream)
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>The details of this response message are as follows:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs">from socket import inet_ntoa

def decode_key_exchange_2_message(session, tcp_dump):
    message = bytes.fromhex(tcp_dump)
    magic, kind, message_body = dissect_message(message)
    assert magic == 0x9910
    assert kind == 0x12
    assert 0x114 &lt;= len(message_body)
    
    unknown_00 = message_body[: 0x10]
    session.target_build = int.from_bytes(message_body[0x10: 0x13], &quot;little&quot;)
    session.target_version = int.from_bytes(message_body[0x13: 0x14], &quot;little&quot;)
    encrypted_target_nonce = message_body[0x14: 0x94]
    encrypted_information = message_body[0x94: 0xac]
    unused = message_body[0xac: ]
    
    cycled_key = itertools.cycle(b&quot;\x10\x99&quot;)
    target_nonce = xor_crypt(encrypted_target_nonce, cycled_key)
    
    key_stream = rc4_variant(target_nonce)
    information = xor_crypt(encrypted_information, key_stream)
    
    recognised_login = information[: 0x10]
    initiator_ip_addr = information[0x10: 0x14]
    netmask = information[0x14: ]
    
    print(f&quot;0008	message_body:&quot;)
    print(f&quot;0008	unknown_00 (marker?)=	{unknown_00}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;0018	target_build?	 = 	{session.target_build}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;001b	target_version?	 = 	{session.target_version}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;001c	target_nonce	  = 	{target_nonce.hex()}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;009c	information:&quot;)
    print(f&quot;009c	recognised_login  = 	{recognised_login}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;00ac	initiator_ip_addr = {inet_ntoa(initiator_ip_addr)}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;00b0	netmask		 = 	{inet_ntoa(netmask)}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;00b4	unused		= 	{unused}&quot;)
    
    session.target_key = derive_key(target_nonce)
    session.target_key_stream = itertools.cycle(session.target_key)

decode_key_exchange_2_message(my_session, &quot;&quot;&quot;
   <span class="hljs-number"> 10 </span>99<span class="hljs-number"> 12 </span>00 1c<span class="hljs-number"> 01 </span>00<span class="hljs-number"> 00 </span><span class="hljs-number"> 78 </span>72<span class="hljs-number"> 74 </span>00<span class="hljs-number"> 83 </span>fa ff ff
   <span class="hljs-number"> 00 </span>00<span class="hljs-number"> 00 </span>00<span class="hljs-number"> 00 </span>00<span class="hljs-number"> 00 </span>00 <span class="hljs-number"> 75 </span>07<span class="hljs-number"> 00 </span>13<span class="hljs-number"> 89 </span>5c<span class="hljs-number"> 69 </span>be
    7f<span class="hljs-number"> 09 </span>60 ed 3d 3d 9c c5  ef<span class="hljs-number"> 55 </span>67<span class="hljs-number"> 29 </span>14<span class="hljs-number"> 67 </span>70 2d
    ef a7 5e bb 5f<span class="hljs-number"> 59 </span>a4 e8 <span class="hljs-number"> 31 </span>d4<span class="hljs-number"> 55 </span>cf f4 7e<span class="hljs-number"> 19 </span>15

<p>Looking at the decoded message, we find references to “XRT” and these reassemble the hardcoded Name “NDISXRPT” that we documented when discussing the <em>NdisRegisterProtocol()</em> call during driver initialization:</p>

</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs">def decode_key_exchange_3_message(session, tcp_dump):
    encrypted_message = bytes.fromhex(tcp_dump)
    message = xor_crypt(encrypted_message, session.target_key_stream)
    magic, kind, message_body = dissect_message(message)
    assert magic == 0x9911
    assert kind == 0x15
    assert 0x94 &lt;= len(message_body)
    
    login = message_body[: 0x10]
    password_hash = message_body[0x10: 0x14]
    initiator_nonce = message_body[0x14: 0x94]
    unused = message_body[0x94: ]
    
    print(f&quot;0008	message_body:&quot;)
    print(f&quot;0008	login			 = {login}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;0018	password_hash	 = {password_hash.hex()}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;001c	initiator_nonce	 = {initiator_nonce.hex()}&quot;)
    print(f&quot;009c	unused		 = {unused}&quot;)
    
    session.initiator_key = derive_key(initiator_nonce)
    session.initiator_key_stream = itertools.cycle(session.initiator_key)
    
    md5 = hashlib.md5()
    md5.update([REDACTED])
    md5.update(session.target_key[: 0x40])
    md5.update(session.initiator_key[0x40: ])
    if (session.target_build &gt; 1410) and (session.target_version &gt;= 16):
        md5.update([REDACTED])
    session.shared_key = md5.digest()

</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-number">0000</span>	magic			 = <span class="hljs-number">9911</span>
<span class="hljs-number">0002</span>	kind			 = <span class="hljs-number">15</span>
<span class="hljs-number">0003</span>	unknown_03		 = <span class="hljs-number">00</span>
<span class="hljs-number">0004</span>	total_length	 = <span class="hljs-number">0000009</span>c
<span class="hljs-number">0008</span>	message_body:
<span class="hljs-number">0008</span>	login			 = b<span class="hljs-comment">&#x27;XRT[REMOVED ZEROS]&#x27;</span>
<span class="hljs-number">0018</span>	password_hash	 = [REDACTED]
<span class="hljs-number">001</span>c	initiator_nonce	 = b0bf1c98[<span class="hljs-comment">REMOVED FOR BREVITY]0bee8fff</span>
<span class="hljs-number">009</span>c	unused		 = b<span class="hljs-comment">&#x27;&#x27;</span>
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>At this point, the peers exchange their nonces and compute two transport keys. Each transport key is used in stream mode to encrypt the TCP half-stream directed towards the side that generated the corresponding nonce.</p>

<p>The peers also combined their nonces into the shared key. This shared key will be used to encrypt the body of each exchanged message, reusing the same key every time.</p>

<p>The final key exchange message confirms that the initiator was successfully authenticated and the backdoor is ready to process instructions:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">dissect_encrypted_message</span>(<span class="hljs-params">transport_key_stream, shared_key, encrypted_message</span>):
    message = xor_crypt(encrypted_message, transport_key_stream)
    magic, kind, encrypted_message_body = dissect_message(message)
    key_stream = rc4_variant(shared_key)
    message_body = xor_crypt(encrypted_message_body, key_stream)
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> magic, kind, message_body

<span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">decode_key_exchange_4_message</span>(<span class="hljs-params">session, tcp_dump</span>):
    encrypted_message = <span class="hljs-built_in">bytes</span>.fromhex(tcp_dump)
    magic, kind, message_body = dissect_encrypted_message(session.initiator_key_stream,
        session.shared_key, encrypted_message)
    <span class="hljs-keyword">assert</span> magic == <span class="hljs-number">0x9911</span>
    <span class="hljs-keyword">assert</span> kind == <span class="hljs-number">0x16</span>
    
    <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;0008	message_body (unused)	 = <span class="hljs-subst">{message_body}</span>&quot;</span>)

</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-number">0000	</span><span class="hljs-string">magic</span>				 <span class="hljs-string">=</span> <span class="hljs-number">9911</span>

</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p class="MsoNoSpacing">For the messages that follow, the <em>kind</em> field encodes the message purpose. This determines the formatting of the message body. For example, the backdoor instruction to set up new connectivity across multiple malicious nodes uses <em>kind</em> value 6 with the following message body structure:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">format_kind_06_message_body</span>(<span class="hljs-params">message_body</span>):

    <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;0008	message_body:&quot;</span>)

    <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;000a	remaining_to_connect	 = <span class="hljs-subst">{remaining_to_connect}</span>&quot;</span>)
    
    offset = <span class="hljs-number">4</span>
    <span class="hljs-keyword">for</span> index <span class="hljs-keyword">in</span> <span class="hljs-built_in">range</span>(number_of_nodes):
        ip_addr = message_body[offset: offset + <span class="hljs-number">4</span>][:: -<span class="hljs-number">1</span>]
        port = <span class="hljs-built_in">int</span>.from_bytes(message_body[offset + <span class="hljs-number">4</span>: offset + <span class="hljs-number">6</span>], <span class="hljs-string">&quot;little&quot;</span>)
        login = message_body[offset + <span class="hljs-number">6</span>: offset + <span class="hljs-number">0x16</span>]
        password = message_body[offset + <span class="hljs-number">0x16</span>: offset + <span class="hljs-number">0x38</span>]
        
        comment = <span class="hljs-string">&quot;&quot;</span>
        <span class="hljs-keyword">if</span> index + remaining_to_connect == number_of_nodes:
            comment = <span class="hljs-string">&quot; (HEAD)&quot;</span>
        <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;<span class="hljs-subst">{offset + <span class="hljs-number">0x08</span>:04x}</span>	node #<span class="hljs-subst">{index + <span class="hljs-number">1</span>}</span><span class="hljs-subst">{comment}</span>:&quot;</span>)
        <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;<span class="hljs-subst">{offset + <span class="hljs-number">0x08</span>:04x}</span>	ip_addr	   = <span class="hljs-subst">{inet_ntoa(ip_addr)}</span>&quot;</span>)
        <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;<span class="hljs-subst">{offset + <span class="hljs-number">0x0c</span>:04x}</span>	port		  = <span class="hljs-subst">{port}</span>&quot;</span>)
        <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;<span class="hljs-subst">{offset + <span class="hljs-number">0x0e</span>:04x}</span>	login		 = <span class="hljs-subst">{login}</span>&quot;</span>)
        <span class="hljs-built_in">print</span>(<span class="hljs-string">f&quot;<span class="hljs-subst">{offset + <span class="hljs-number">0x1e</span>:04x}</span>	password	 = <span class="hljs-subst">{password}</span>&quot;</span>)

</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>We could continue to decrypt all the backdoor communication that follows:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs">message_body_formatters = {

<span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">decode_encrypted_initiator_message</span>(<span class="hljs-params">session, tcp_dump</span>):

        session.shared_key, encrypted_message)

</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-number">0000</span>	magic					 = <span class="hljs-number">9911</span>

<span class="hljs-number">007c</span>		node #<span class="hljs-number">3</span>:
<span class="hljs-number">007c</span>			ip_addr		 = <span class="hljs-number">10.0</span><span class="hljs-number">.4</span><span class="hljs-number">.4</span>

<span class="hljs-number">0082</span>			login			 = b&#x27;XRT[REMOVED ZEROS]&#x27;
<span class="hljs-number">0092</span>			password		 = b&#x27;[REDACTED]&#x27;
<span class="hljs-number">00b4</span>		unused				= b&#x27;&#x27;
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p class="MsoNoSpacing">The most interesting observation about encryption is that Daxin supports two methods for computing the shared key during the key exchange. To select which of the two methods to use, the initiator examines the target message, comparing what looks like build and version numbers against certain hardcoded constants. This could be in order to facilitate upgrading the malicious network in the field without disruption.</p>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing">Additionally, the current key exchange implementation involves additional obfuscation that is not present in some older samples. It is possible that the attacker was forced to change the algorithm and decided to implement additional measures to protect the details of new logic.</p>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing">An alternative explanation is that different teams within the attacker organization were sharing the same codebase, where one of these teams implemented the alternative key exchange method and related obfuscations to mitigate against potential compromise due to the other team’s activity, while still sharing some of the communication infrastructure.</p>

<h2>External communication</h2>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing">The communications protocol documented in the previous section is how two backdoor instances communicate with each other.</p>

<p>On top of that, the analyzed sample also supports two additional communication methods. These additional methods are well suited for crossing the perimeter of the target organization.</p>

<p><strong>HTTP</strong></p>

<p>One of these additional communication methods uses HTTP messages to encapsulate backdoor communications.</p>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing">To demonstrate this, we implemented our own client to interact with the backdoor using this method. Our client communicated with the backdoor instance running on “Alice-PC” over HTTP to control a set of infected machines, as discussed in the previous two sections.</p>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing">On the target computer, in case it is infected with a copy of Daxin, the first HTTP request causes the TCP connection to be hijacked due to the malicious packet filter triggering on the HTTP “POST” method string with URI substring “756981520337” as explained in the “Networking” section of our previous blog.</p>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing">Daxin then parses HTTP request headers and extracts the request body. The request body is then interpreted using the same logic as already described in the “Communications protocol” section.</p>

<p>The reverse communication is then encapsulated as the HTTP response body. When generating the HTTP response, the malicious driver includes “SID” cookie. The value of “SID” cookie is then used when constructing the URI for the subsequent HTTP request.</p>

<p class="MsoNoSpacing"><strong>“HOST” connectivity</strong></p>

<p>The malicious driver can also be configured to communicate with a remote TCP server for command and control. It then periodically connects to the remote server, performs a handshake that is unique to this connectivity method, and then starts backdoor communication.</p>

<p>This connectivity method is controlled with persistent configuration that can be updated by the remote attacker, as explained in the “Backdoor capabilities” section of our previous blog.</p>

<p>To obtain the details of the TCP server to connect to, Daxin checks the value of the “HOST” configuration item. In case the value starts with “http://”, the TCP server details are retrieved from the remote web server, as described below. Otherwise, the configuration value is interpreted as the TCP server address and port.</p>

<p>In order to retrieve the TCP server details from the remote web server, the analyzed sample contacts the provided URL and scans the received HTTP response, including HTTP headers, for magic strings. Whenever it finds “f8xD4C01” followed later by “d7C6x12B”, it attempts to interpret any data immediately following the first marker as a hexadecimal string. The data obtained by decoding the hexadecimal string is then decrypted using the following algorithm:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-keyword">def</span> decrypt(data):
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> bytes([((<span class="hljs-keyword">byte</span> - <span class="hljs-number">0</span>x7d) &amp; <span class="hljs-number">0</span>xff) ^ <span class="hljs-number">0</span>x49 <span class="hljs-keyword">for</span> <span class="hljs-keyword">byte</span> in data])
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>The decrypted data are interpreted as the TCP server address and port to use.</p>

<p>Whenever the analyzed sample connects to the TCP server, it sends the following sequence of bytes as its handshake:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-variable">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">serialize_client_handshake</span>(<span class="hljs-variable">tags_value</span>):
    <span class="hljs-variable">magic</span> <span class="hljs-operator">=</span> <span class="hljs-variable">b</span><span class="hljs-string">&quot;<span class="hljs-char escape_">\xA8</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\xB0</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x13</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x7C</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x00</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x2C</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x13</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\xBA</span>&quot;</span>
    <span class="hljs-variable">build</span> <span class="hljs-operator">=</span> <span class="hljs-number">1909</span>
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> <span class="hljs-variable">magic</span> <span class="hljs-operator">+</span> <span class="hljs-variable">struct</span>.<span class="hljs-property">pack</span>(<span class="hljs-string">&quot;&lt;HH&quot;</span>, <span class="hljs-variable">build</span>, <span class="hljs-title function_">len</span>(<span class="hljs-variable">tags_value</span>)) <span class="hljs-operator">+</span> <span class="hljs-variable">tags_value</span>
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>The <em>tags_value </em>parameter is the value of the “TAGS” configuration item. We suspect that the remote server uses the <em>tags_value</em> for tracking specific infections.</p>

<p>The analyzed sample then expects to receive the following hardcoded sequence of bytes from the remote server:</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-code-block"><div class="code-block__source"><code class="hljs"><span class="hljs-variable">def</span> <span class="hljs-title function_">serialize_server_handshake</span>():
    <span class="hljs-keyword">return</span> <span class="hljs-variable">b</span><span class="hljs-string">&quot;<span class="hljs-char escape_">\xA8</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\xB0</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x13</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x7C</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x45</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\x1B</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\xAC</span><span class="hljs-char escape_">\xC0</span>&quot;</span>
</code></div></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>This should be followed by the usual key exchange as described in the “Communication protocol” section, where the remote server acts as initiator.</p>

<h2>Conclusion</h2>

<p>This concludes the second and final part of our technical analysis of Backdoor.Daxin.</p>

computing the shared key during the key exchange. To select which of the two methods to use, the initiator examines the target message, comparing what looks like build and version numbers against certain hardcoded constants. This could be in order to facilitate upgrading the malicious network in the field without disruption.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp class=\"MsoNoSpacing\"\u003eAdditionally, the current key exchange implementation involves additional obfuscation that is not present in some older samples. It is possible that the attacker was forced to change the algorithm and decided to implement additional measures to protect the details of new logic.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp class=\"MsoNoSpacing\"\u003eAn alternative explanation is that different teams within the attacker organization were sharing the same codebase, where one of these teams implemented the alternative key exchange method and related obfuscations to mitigate against potential compromise due to the other team’s activity, while still sharing some of the communication infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eExternal communication\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp class=\"MsoNoSpacing\"\u003eThe communications protocol documented in the previous section is how two backdoor instances communicate with each other.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eOn top of that, the analyzed sample also supports two additional communication methods. These additional methods are well suited for crossing the perimeter of the target organization.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHTTP\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eOne of these additional communication methods uses HTTP messages to encapsulate backdoor communications.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp class=\"MsoNoSpacing\"\u003eTo demonstrate this, we implemented our own client to interact with the backdoor using this method. Our client communicated with the backdoor instance running on “Alice-PC” over HTTP to control a set of infected machines, as discussed in the previous two sections.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp class=\"MsoNoSpacing\"\u003eOn the target computer, in case it is infected with a copy of Daxin, the first HTTP request causes the TCP connection to be hijacked due to the malicious packet filter triggering on the HTTP “POST” method string with URI substring “756981520337” as explained in the “Networking” section of our previous blog.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp class=\"MsoNoSpacing\"\u003eDaxin then parses HTTP request headers and extracts the request body. The request body is then interpreted using the same logic as already described in the “Communications protocol” section.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThe reverse communication is then encapsulated as the HTTP response body. When generating the HTTP response, the malicious driver includes “SID” cookie. The value of “SID” cookie is then used when constructing the URI for the subsequent HTTP request.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp class=\"MsoNoSpacing\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e“HOST” connectivity\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThe malicious driver can also be configured to communicate with a remote TCP server for command and control. It then periodically connects to the remote server, performs a handshake that is unique to this connectivity method, and then starts backdoor communication.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThis connectivity method is controlled with persistent configuration that can be updated by the remote attacker, as explained in the “Backdoor capabilities” section of our previous blog.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eTo obtain the details of the TCP server to connect to, Daxin checks the value of the “HOST” configuration item. Otherwise, the configuration value is interpreted as the TCP server address and port.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eIn order to retrieve the TCP server details from the remote web server, the analyzed sample contacts the provided URL and scans the received HTTP response, including HTTP headers, for magic strings. Whenever it finds “f8xD4C01” followed later by “d7C6x12B”, it attempts to interpret any data immediately following the first marker as a hexadecimal string. The backdoor was downloaded from two servers belonging to the Backblaze cloud storage company:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003egitempire.s3.us-east-005.backblazeb2.com\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eelvenforest.s3.us-east-005.backblazeb2.com\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Donald Gay certificate was also used to sign a sample from the malware family we call Stagecomp and which downloads the Darkcomp\u0026nbsp;backdoor. The Stagecomp and the Darkcomp malware have been linked to Seedworm by vendors including Google, Microsoft and Kaspersky. While this malware wasn’t seen on the targeted networks, the use of the same certificates suggests the same actor - namely Seedworm - was behind the activity on the networks of the U.S. companies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile it’s not known if the operations of Seedworm are disrupted by the current conflict, already having a presence on U.S. and Israeli networks prior to the current hostilities beginning means the threat group is in a potentially dangerous position to launch attacks. While we have disrupted these breaches, other organizations could still be vulnerable to attack.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSeedworm is a long-standing Iranian threat group, which usually mounts classic espionage attacks for the purposes of spying and information gathering. Active since 2017, CISA has said that Seedworm is “\u003ca href=\"https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa22-055a\"\u003ea subordinate element within the Iranian Ministry of Intelligence and Security\u003c/a\u003e (MOIS).” Seedworm originally focused on victims in the Middle East but later broadened its scope to target telecommunications, defense, local government, and oil and natural gas organizations in Asia, Africa, Europe, and North America. The group develops its own custom malware as well as using dual-use and living off the land tools.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eContext\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn February 28, 2026, the U.S. and Israel launched a coordinated offensive military air operation targeting Iran, leading to the death of Iran’s Supreme Leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei, who was apparently killed on March 1 when a U.S./Israeli airstrike hit his compound. Several other high-ranking Iranian officials, as well as multiple civilians, were also killed in strikes.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn retaliation, Iran launched drones and ballistic missiles at adversaries throughout the Gulf region, including targeting Israel and U.S. military and diplomatic outposts in multiple countries in the region.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBecause of the heated tension in the region and ongoing attacks, it is likely Iran and its allies may also initiate cyber operations to further target their adversaries. Both Israel and Iran have a history of carrying out destructive cyberattacks, including against each other. While internet access in Iran may be disrupted by current military operations, there are cyber operatives working for the regime based in other countries.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe UK’s National Cyber Security Centre\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.ncsc.gov.uk/news/ncsc-advises-uk-organisations-take-action-following-conflict-in-middle-east\"\u003ereleased an alert\u003c/a\u003e following this recent activity, stating that “Iranian state and Iran-linked cyber actors almost certainly currently maintain at least some capability to conduct cyber activity” and warning about the potential threat posed by “Iran-linked hacktivists”. Check Point also reported recently that the Handala threat group (see below)\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.firstpost.com/tech/how-irans-handala-hackers-are-using-elon-musks-starlink-for-cyberattacks-amid-internet-blackout-ws-e-13985806.html\"\u003ehas been using the Starlink satellite network\u003c/a\u003e to stay online even before this most recent activity began, with the group reportedly leveraging the technology since mid-January, when a nationwide Internet shutdown was announced by Iran’s government.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eExamining the cyber activity typically carried out by threat actors associated with Iran and its allies may help us predict the kinds of cyber operations we may see being executed as this conflict continues.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIranian threat actors have become increasingly proficient in recent years. Not only has their tooling and malware improved, but they’ve also demonstrated strong social engineering capabilities, including spear-phishing campaigns and “honeytrap” operations used to build relationships with targets of interest to gain access to accounts or sensitive information.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne of the hallmarks of Iran’s operations in cyberspace is that it periodically mounts destructive attacks against organizations in countries it deems hostile, which at the moment would obviously include the U.S. and Israel. That creates a risk for organizations in those countries because these attacks are about sending a message rather than stealing information, which means that any organization in the country targeted could be in the firing line.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOther recent activity\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDoxing Israeli officials and regional energy sector participants\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eHandala is an Iranian-aligned hacktivist group that is also known to operate in support of Palestine. They have been active since at least 2024. They are known for conducting attacks targeting Israeli organizations and entities perceived to support Israel by conducting phishing attacks, data theft, ransomware, extortion and destructive attacks, including the use of custom wipers. The group operates a leaks site where victim names are posted alongside stolen data and messages from the group. The group was also reportedly active on multiple underground cybercrime forums including BreachForums, Ramp and Exploit during its early days, but has since become more active on Telegram channels and X (formerly Twitter).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn December 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.kelacyber.com/blog/handala-hack-telegram-breach-israeli-officials\"\u003ethe group claimed to have compromised\u003c/a\u003e the mobile devices of former Israeli Prime Minister Naftali Bennett and Benjamin Netanyahu's Chief of Staff, Tzachi Braverman. The group leaked material they said they had stolen from the phones, including the contact information of prominent Israeli officials, journalists and business people, photos and videos. However,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.kelacyber.com/blog/handala-hack-telegram-breach-israeli-officials\"\u003eanalysis by researchers\u003c/a\u003e disputed some of these claims, saying that the attacks appeared to be limited to Telegram accounts, and did not achieve complete phone access.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn February 2026, Handala\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.timesofisrael.com/iran-linked-hacker-group-claims-to-breach-data-of-israels-largest-healthcare-network/\"\u003eclaimed to have breached one of Israel's largest healthcare networks\u003c/a\u003e. Meanwhile, in March 2026, the group said it had breached Sharjah National Oil Corporation and Israel Opportunity Energy, exfiltrating more than 1.3TB of sensitive data, including confidential financial data, oil contracts and project details. The group has also made claims about breaching Saudi Arabian energy company Saudi Aramco in a post on its leaks site. However, the documents shared appeared to consist of older files that were already in circulation previously. This raises the possibility that the claim may have been exaggerated or partially fabricated, potentially representing an influence or psychological operation intended to generate attention, panic or reputation damage. The group has also posted messages claiming that Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu will be their next target.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eSpearphishing academics and NGOs for intelligence collection\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://www.group-ib.com/blog/muddywater-espionage\"\u003eIn an October 2025 campaign\u003c/a\u003e, Seedworm carried out a sophisticated spear-phishing attack that used a compromised mailbox to distribute a custom backdoor known as Phoenix to international organizations across the Middle East and North Africa (MENA), targeting more than 100 government entities as part of an espionage campaign.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers leveraged a malicious Office attachment that has technical overlap with previously reported Seedworm attacks to deliver Phoenix. The command \u0026amp; control (C\u0026amp;C) server also reportedly hosted the PDQ remote access tool, which was used for remote access and persistence, as well as a custom browser credential stealer. It is believed the motive behind these attacks was intelligence collection, as well as persistent access, for the purposes of longer-term espionage and exfiltration.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eElsewhere, in November 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.proofpoint.com/uk/blog/threat-insight/crossed-wires-case-study-iranian-espionage-and-attribution\"\u003eSeedworm was also linked to attacks that targeted academics with expertise on the Middle East and other foreign policy experts\u003c/a\u003e. This activity took place between June and August 2025. Suspicious spear-phishing emails impersonated Suzanne Maloney – the vice president and director of the Foreign Policy program at the Brookings Institution and an expert on Iran – using a Gmail address and a misspelled version of her name - “Suzzane Maloney.”. In the attacks, the actors started out using a benign email, which eventually led to a subsequent email that contained a malicious link to a remote access tool payload. It is likely these attacks were carried out as a means to perform espionage - more specifically, as a means to gather intelligence that could be leveraged for strategic advantage.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThese attacks had TTP overlaps with other Iranian aligned groups (Smoke Sandstorm, Mint Sandstorm/Charming Kitten) but were subsequently attributed to Seedworm.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOther 2025 activity\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCamera scanning for intelligence gathering\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eMarshtreader (Pink Sandstorm, Agrius, Agonizing Serpens) is a group that has been active since 2020 and is reportedly linked to Iran’s Ministry of Intelligence and Security (MOIS). It is known for its destructive operations against countries in the Middle East, specifically Israel, conducting attacks under multiple aliases and leveraging data leaks in order to control and shape narratives using wiper and fake ransomware malware.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn June 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://blog.checkpoint.com/research/what-defenders-need-to-know-about-irans-cyber-capabilities\"\u003eit was reported\u003c/a\u003e that the group was observed scanning for vulnerable cameras using CVE-2023-6895 and CVE-2017-7921 across Israel during the June 2025 conflict using infrastructure associated with Iranian actors.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn previous conflicts, actors have been observed compromising cameras to gather intelligence to support bombing damage assessment (BDA) by providing near-real time visibility of impact from bombings and strikes. It is likely these attacks were conducted to gain similar visibility into sensitive locations to perform reconnaissance and potentially enable follow-on targeting of high value targets.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAdditionally, in June 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/2025-the-untold-stories-of-check-point-research/\"\u003ea successful password-spraying attack\u003c/a\u003e conducted from Nord VPN infrastructure against Israeli municipal government entities was reported followed by spear-phishing attacks that contained links to a ClickFix page designed to trick users into executing malicious PowerShell to ultimately deliver a remote access tool (RAT) that can execute arbitrary commands by the attackers. It is likely the motive behind these attacks was account compromise and espionage. It is not clear what actor was behind these attacks, but the targeting of Israeli targets points to an Iranian actor as the most likely perpetrator.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDieNet DDoS attacks\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDieNet is a pro-Palestinian hacktivist group that emerged on Telegram around March 2025 and announced its intention to target “outlaw sites and corrupt government platforms” using DDoS attacks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFollowing the arrest of activist Mahmoud Khalil, its activities intensified, with the group\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.cisecurity.org/insights/blog/hacktivist-group-dienet-claims-ddos-attacks-against-u-s-c-n-i\"\u003eclaiming responsibility\u003c/a\u003e for multiple DDoS attacks against U.S. critical infrastructure, including energy, financial, healthcare, government, transit and communication systems.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn its attacks, the group leveraged high-volume DDoS attacks reportedly via DDoS-as-a-service infrastructure, including TCP RST, DNS amplification, TCP SYN floods and NTP amplification attacks, as well as website defacements and data breaches.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBased on reporting, its motives were likely political retaliation and service disruption.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat can we expect next?\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eGiven Iran's history of attacks leveraging destructive wipers, distributed-denial-of-service (DDoS) and hack-and-leak attacks, the likely next steps for the nation’s cyber actors and supporters may be multiple campaigns combining high-visibility disruption for political signaling and lower-visibility access operations for strategic leverage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDefenders should anticipate noisy activity such as DDoS attacks, defacements, and leak claims targeting government, transportation, energy and defense contractors to amplify psychological and economic pressure.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is also likely that more capable state-aligned groups will continue credential harvesting operations, along with vulnerability exploitation and covert persistence against critical infrastructure to generate immediate impact, while also positioning themselves for potential future destructive, espionage or coercive operations.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDDoS and defacements\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eGiven the increase in \"hacktivist\" activity, we predict a surge in DDoS and defacements for fast signaling and media impact, similar to what has been observed recently with Handala’s claims of targeting critical national infrastructure (CNI).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is likely such attacks would target a range of sectors, including government portals, municipal sites, airports/ports, logistics providers, banks, telcos, media and symbolic brands.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePassword spraying and mailbox compromises\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eOver the last year, multiple reports involving Iran-backed groups repeatedly highlighted credential attacks and mailbox compromises as a means of initial access and intelligence gathering.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTargets could include defense organizations, government, contractors and NGOs. Additionally, adjacent organizations that support base operations, including fuel, catering, logistics, and communications could also be targets of these attacks.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eLeaks / intimidation operations / psyops\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eHacktivists such as Handala repeatedly use leaks and claims to amplify fear and pressure even when access is only partial - this is key escalation behavior.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePotential targets of these kinds of attacks and claims would likely include healthcare, local government, airports/ports, transportation and education, as well as high-profile individuals tied to defense, politics and media.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCritical infrastructure and opportunistic attacks\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eGiven the current escalations between the U.S. and Iran, it is likely that CNI is at high risk of attack, as well as organizations supporting these entities.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations with exposed terminal operating systems, schedules and trucking/rail interfaces may be targeted, as well as passenger processing systems, baggage systems, and contractor networks. Additionally, given the high risk, other organizations that operate within sectors such as energy/fuel supply chains may be targets.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDestructive attacks\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIran has previously exhibited high capabilities in destructive potential, particularly during escalation windows.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAny attacks would likely to be focused on energy and utilities, transportation and logistics, financial sector, telecoms, healthcare, defense contractors and military suppliers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow can defenders prepare?\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations should prepare by focusing on strengthening monitoring capabilities and ensure resilience across their infrastructure where possible. Early indicators such as vulnerability scanning, credential attacks and reconnaissance activity often provide an opportunity for defenders to detect intrusion attempts early in the attack chain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDDoS and defacement campaigns\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDue to the likelihood of early retaliation and intensifying psyops, defenders should expect attempts to disrupt public-facing services and monitor any internet-facing infrastructure for the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9907433f271915689c1a8bf019d5e62b\"\u003eSpikes in HTTP requests from large, distributed IP ranges\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e96427b7a90e5da0e984af3f8c0fdd136\"\u003eRepeated probing of admin portals\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5e00b81dc19d0213a0927459f8aa3503\"\u003eExploit attempts targeting web frameworks and content management systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee6934ad365e30a6db855c77a020ab770\"\u003eScanning activity against exposed API endpoints\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo prepare, organizations should look at performing the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec76d32596b774764f73dba74c0de99fb\"\u003eDeploy web application firewalls (WAF) with updated rule sets\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1e15bf4aa2f26bf33c37f4461d24c983\"\u003eEnable DDoS protection via CDN or upstream filtering services\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edee0f3fe7529f24ccea7061d72a38c0f\"\u003eDecommission any non-essential or unused publicly accessible services\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e61c448683b9b01d84672d644277a277d\"\u003eEnsure all up-to-date patches for web applications/plugins are applied regularly\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e292625cb84849c75a9c01dab5091fe9e\"\u003eEnsure website backups exist for rapid restoration, if required\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2962068e78c7b8fb76d38777f9d19c92\"\u003eMonitor underground forums, Telegram channels and social media for claims involving your organization\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCredential attacks\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eCredential attacks are one of the most common initial access techniques used by Iranian-linked groups, which include attack attempts against multiple public-facing services.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDefenders should ensure monitoring is in place to identify patterns consistent with password-spraying attempts, such as the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e427180b681ed7588311582b71ca8c753\"\u003eRepeated login failure attempts across multiple users\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef4ff03b0e9f9454d42c7244b35dfa353\"\u003eAuthentication attempts from unusual geographic locations\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5b6a6023a07ca5a0ac1f477d3ac98ab9\"\u003eMFA fatigue attacks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4f166e292d6f50afecd7b56e263882c6\"\u003eLogin attempts occurring outside of normal working hours\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eaab2500c0fbde3ff6fd9cc19b38f8224\"\u003eVulnerability scanning and exploitation of vulnerable VPN appliances or edge infrastructure\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e31e40cd56aed39c23f15461e47c3e149\"\u003eDeployment of web shells on internet-facing servers\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee38a332901f0550436234911a4866b88\"\u003eCredential harvesting through phishing campaigns\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations should review and harden any identity security mechanisms by performing the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e301b4b1a364cf4ac999c3b8bc3094b49\"\u003eEnable multi-factor authentication for all remote access\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e620c417d33094b4eedd976309b4e74c5\"\u003eDisable legacy authentication protocols\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb4140b4818500b468a0321318fb72564\"\u003eImplement condition access policies based on location and device risk\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb0e10bd802f63a9907669761c2e1fadd\"\u003eRestrict admin logins to specific locations, where possible\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed04ca6098625b3b8a86c95978f25436a\"\u003eMonitor identity provider logs for any anomalies\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eLeak campaigns and intimidation operations\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eHacktivist groups often use hack-and-leak campaigns designed to gain media attention and apply psychological pressure, usually via partial data leaks and exaggerated breach claims. Security teams should watch for indicators of data staging or exfiltration, such as the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3766d43b006a17cb769b7aab48df1fd3\"\u003eUnusual downloads from email systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3609cdd61bdf12fce06fb3e6cc5c16ff\"\u003eUnusual access to document repositories\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef7a5d98efc74600869e0324e3edf801d\"\u003eSuspicious archive creation (e.g. ZIP, RAR) on internal systems, usually involving collection of multiple file types\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0e1b93d8bd0dd1ff29f11e3df3ffbac0\"\u003eLarge outbound data transfers to external cloud storage platforms\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecd27e6779ad8457382341057c769c81a\"\u003eUnexpected use of data-transfer applications (e.g. Rclone) in their environment\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations should focus on ensuring monitoring is in place for the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee3551bc0a91088fa3fe63a7590b745f0\"\u003eLarge outbound data transfers\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec8642a82c5bdf9172c512ab434a02e36\"\u003eImplement data loss prevention (DLP) policies\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e00dca864986c76de833162094b42a525\"\u003eRestrict access to external cloud storage platforms\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef999d269ec8e71f47b7c2bc4e432a6cb\"\u003eEnable auditing of email and file access\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eHaving a communications plan for potential leak claims can also help organizations respond quickly to these threats.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAttacks on critical infrastructure\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eCritical infrastructure organizations and companies that support military logistics may face attacks that attempt to compromise the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e27261a57ab62b373b187aae3f8dc5d64\"\u003eOperational Technology (OT) interfaces\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4cab9c9dd0b4aab7f5cbbb01dce1d47a\"\u003eScheduled and logistics systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef05146c4632aec806b486190a00ff764\"\u003eContractor networks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0d42aeeac5d715a1088109418b40a8e3\"\u003eRemote management systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eSecurity teams should ensure adequate monitoring is in place for:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eedcc057d48eebaf843f7b545fd344e8d\"\u003eAbnormal access to ICS\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6c3122c28ccc64563981301738cce9e8\"\u003eUnexpected remote connections to operational networks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e8b634b5d24dce0f167024656a457be31\"\u003eUnusual configuration changes in critical systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea353694c59e79ad13c77cb0dd1d30585\"\u003eVendor access and contractor networks\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations faced with these attacks, at a minimum, should ensure:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7dc9e975de678bde2af4fb6ed093d701\"\u003eNetwork segmentation across operational technology networks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e47eef2e782a684d7d537edc6f90f1f97\"\u003eRestrict remote access to infrastructure systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli of critical configuration systems\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDestructive attacks\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIran has repeatedly demonstrated its destructive capabilities in the past, with\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/shamoon-back-destructive\"\u003eattacks such as Shamoon\u003c/a\u003e, which targeted Saudi Arabia's oil industry to wipe thousands of systems.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations that anticipate such attacks should ensure they monitor for indicators that attackers may be preparing for a destructive operation such as:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4b4b06b075e6ec1d667443fd2ef05ce5\"\u003eMass scheduled task creation\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea27e0611d11edcfb2bb916354ea134ab\"\u003eAttempts to disable security applications\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ead18a4d48690724886a628851fe3be1f\"\u003eDeletion of shadow copies or backup data\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e848ab767fc6f0e5a439dc0d2f5d84590\"\u003eUnusual administrative commands executed across multiple hosts\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations should prioritize resilience against destructive attacks by conducting the following tasks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1347f374bc0e4986c1ead6cb9929909f\"\u003eIsolating backup infrastructure from production networks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3a03bd5e96152b8854cbc9d08d2e5a3e\"\u003eEnable immutable backups\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efaeab571a48e6b510a071e1920c94f59\"\u003eTest disaster recovery procedures regularly\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eEnsuring systems can be restored quickly is critical to recovering from the impact of destructive attacks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHistorical activity\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStuxnet\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne of the most infamous cyber incidents to ever take place in the Middle East region was the deployment of the Stuxnet worm, which was designed to break laboratory equipment used by Iranian scientists to enrich uranium at the Natanz facility in Iran. Iran has claimed that this facility\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.reuters.com/world/middle-east/no-sign-irans-nuclear-sites-were-hit-iaea-says-iran-alleges-one-was-2026-03-02/\"\u003ehas been hit in strikes by Israel and the U.S. in recent days\u003c/a\u003e. The disruption of Iran’s nuclear program to prevent the country from developing nuclear weapons was one of the reasons given by the U.S. administration for carrying out these recent strikes. The facility was also hit in U.S. strikes in June 2025, which were believed at the time to have rendered the facility inoperable.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStuxnet was among the first known major nation-state cyberattacks that demonstrated hackers’ ability to manipulate and even destroy physical equipment. Stuxnet was designed to cause the spinning motors at the bottom of Natanz's enrichment centrifuges to shatter. It was first\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://docs.broadcom.com/doc/security-response-w32-stuxnet-dossier-11-en\"\u003epublished about by researchers at Symantec in 2010\u003c/a\u003e, after the worm spread outside of the Natanz facility and was found on private networks. Given that Stuxnet was only discovered after penetrating private networks, it is quite possible that cyber operations are currently being leveraged by and against infrastructure that we know nothing about - yet.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReports last year indicated potential cyber warfare impacting the region then too, including an attack by pro-Israel hackers dubbed Predatory Sparrow on Iranian crypto exchange Nobitex in which\u003ca href=\"https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/pro-israel-hackers-hit-irans-nobitex-exchange-burn-90m-in-crypto/\"\u003e the attackers drained $90 million of cryptocurrency\u003c/a\u003e from the exchange. There were also\u003ca href=\"https://thehackernews.com/2025/06/iranian-apt35-hackers-targeting-israeli.html\"\u003e reports\u003c/a\u003e that Iranian group Damselfly was carrying out a targeted phishing campaign focused on high-profile Israeli individuals, particularly prominent academics, journalists, and security researchers (\u003cem\u003eSee more in Damselfly profile\u003c/em\u003e).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDamselfly is just one of the key cyber actors who may be active in the current conflict, potentially targeting the networks of significant institutions in other nations for espionage, disruptive or destructive purposes.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOther key actors\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDruidfly\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDruidfly (aka Homeland Justice, Karma) is an Iranian attack group that specializes in disk-wiping attacks. It first came to public attention after a July 2022 wiper attack on multiple targets belonging to the government of Albania. The wiper left messages directed against the Mujahideen E-Khalq (MEK), an Iranian dissident organization based in Albania. Shortly afterward, a group calling itself Homeland Justice claimed credit for the attack.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;In response to the attack, Albania broke off diplomatic relations with Iran. This triggered another wave of attacks in September 2022, apparently in retaliation for Albania publicly attributing the attacks to Iran. While Homeland Justice purported to be a hacktivist outfit, the FBI later established that “Iranian state cyber actors” were responsible for the attacks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDruidfly reappeared in 2023, when it began targeting Israel with a wiper called BibiWiper, seemingly named after Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, whose nickname is “Bibi” \u003cem\u003e(See Case Study)\u003c/em\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://x.com/threatintel/status/1936049254432231444\"\u003eOn June 20, 2025, when hostilities between Iran and Israel were previously at a high, we tweeted that we had seen a Druidfly wiper targeting organizations in Albania\u003c/a\u003e. The wiper was signed with a legitimate certificate, which was probably stolen. On the Monday following (June 23),\u003ca href=\"https://therecord.media/tirana-albania-government-cyberattack-iran-linked-group\"\u003e it was reported in the media that public services in Albania’s capital Tirana had been disrupted by a cyber attack\u003c/a\u003e that took down the city’s official website and affected local government operations. Homeland Justice claimed credit for the attack and said it had taken down the city’s official website, exfiltrated data and wiped servers, citing the presence of MEK in the country as the reason for the attack.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCase study: Druidfly attacks on Israeli targets\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFollowing the escalation of the conflict in Gaza in 2023, Druidfly was linked to a series of wiper attacks against multiple targets in Israel. In this case, the attacks were carried out under a persona called Karma that purports to be a hacktivist group sympathetic to the Palestinian cause.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe wiper deployed in these attacks was called BibiWiper, seemingly named after Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, whose nickname is Bibi. The wiper encrypted files on the hard disk before overwriting the master boot record (MBR) and crashing the computer. Efforts to restart the computer would fail because of the destruction of the MBR. Analysis of the wiper revealed clear anti-Israel messages within the wiper’s code.\u003c/p\u003e"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"2a:T1f04,"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"\u003cp\u003eFurthermore, analysis of BibiWiper by the Threat Hunter Team found clear similarities between it and wipers deployed by Druidfly during attacks against Albania in 2022 and 2023.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTracing other tools used to initiate the BibiWiper attacks against Israel revealed the following overlap in tactics, techniques, and procedures between these attacks and earlier Druidfly attacks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eaeeb00d1b3fee85f1764530d8616b71b\"\u003eHTTPSnoop malware was previously deployed prior to the Druidfly wiping attacks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e8813c16ba2ffe79b17c2f805d344a979\"\u003eUse of the remote desktop tools AnyDesk and ScreenConnect\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e27e5de1cfb083dac4c58a312a596b7d7\"\u003eUse of ReGeorg web shells\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDamselfly\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDamselfly (aka Charming Kitten, Mint Sandstorm) is an Iranian espionage group that has been active since 2014. It was initially known for targeting Israel with attacks before it broadened its focus to include the U.S. and other countries. While the group is principally known to be involved in intelligence gathering, members of the group are also known to have participated in extortion attacks. It has been linked by multiple vendors with Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn March 2022, Damselfly was\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://go.recordedfuture.com/hubfs/reports/cta-2022-0330.pdf\"\u003eone of several Iranian groups reported to have moved into mounting large-scale social engineering campaigns\u003c/a\u003e. Consistent features of these campaigns included the use of charismatic sock puppets, lures of prospective job opportunities, solicitation by journalists, and masquerading as think tank experts seeking opinions. The attackers leveraged networks such as LinkedIn, Facebook, Twitter, and Google.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDamselfly has also been linked to an attack targeting a nuclear security expert at a U.S.-based think tank in July 2023; attacks on Israel’s transportation, logistics, and technology sectors in November 2023; as well as a January 2024 campaign targeting individuals working on Middle Eastern affairs at universities and research organizations in Belgium, France, Gaza, Israel, the UK, and the U.S. The attackers in that campaign used bespoke phishing lures themed around the Israel-Hamas conflict to trick targets into downloading malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn 2025,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://research.checkpoint.com/2025/iranian-educated-manticore-targets-leading-tech-academics/\"\u003eCheck Point reported\u003c/a\u003e that a new Damselfly campaign that began in mid-June 2025 targeted Israeli journalists, cyber security experts and computer science professors from leading Israeli universities with spear phishing campaigns in an attempt to steal credentials and multi-factor authentication codes in order to gain access to targets’ email accounts. Some of the victims were approached by attackers who posed as fictitious assistants to technology executives or researchers through emails and WhatsApp messages.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eMantis\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eActive since at least 2014, Mantis (aka Desert Falcon, Arid Viper, APT-C-23), is an Arabic speaking group that appears to be based in the Gaza Strip. The group is known to mount espionage attacks against targets in the government, military, media, financial, research, education, and energy sectors. Most of its attacks have been against organizations in the Middle East but it has, on occasion, attacked targets outside the region. It has also been known on occasion to target individuals or organizations internally within Gaza. While\u003ca href=\"https://www.cybereason.com/blog/operation-bearded-barbie-apt-c-23-campaign-targeting-israeli-officials\"\u003e other vendors have linked the group to Hamas\u003c/a\u003e, the Threat Hunter Team cannot make a definitive attribution to any Palestinian organization.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe group mainly favors spear-phishing emails with malicious attachments or links to malicious files as its main infection vector. Mantis uses custom malware and its most recent toolset includes the backdoors Trojan.Micropsia and Trojan.AridGopher. Micropsia is capable of taking screenshots, keylogging, and archiving certain file types using WinRAR in preparation for data exfiltration. However, its main purpose appears to be running secondary payloads for the attackers. Arid Gopher is a modular backdoor that is written in Go. It appears to be regularly updated and rewritten by the attackers, most likely to evade detection.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThese tools were used in a Mantis attack in late 2022/early 2023 that targeted organizations within the Palestinian territories. The initial infection vector for this campaign remains unknown, but both the Micropsia and AridGopher malware were deployed in this attack. In one intrusion, the attackers deployed three distinct versions of the same toolset (that is, different variants of the same tools) on three groups of computers. Compartmentalizing the attack in this fashion was likely a precautionary measure. If one toolset was discovered, the attackers would still have a persistent presence on the target’s network.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise (IOCs)\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e0f9cf1cf8d641562053ce533aaa413754db88e60404cab6bbaa11f2b2491d542 -
